Like during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even book club—I just can’t seem to sit there and listen the whole time. I do that a lot. If I overhear junior detectives talking through a lead, or even a neighbor chatting in the hallway, I’ll jump in with questions or advice before anyone asks me. Then afterward I realize I kind of barged into someone else’s conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself intruding on others physically, such as cutting in line or interrupting people's activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do intrude on people’s activities, mostly by jumping into conversations. Like if I hear junior detectives talking through a lead, or a neighbor talking in the hallway, I’ll cut in with questions or advice before they’ve asked me. Then afterward I realize I barged into something that wasn’t really my conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty waiting your turn in lines or queues?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Lines can annoy me, but I can’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern of having trouble waiting my turn in queues specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a major issue for me. I mean, some days are messy, like anyone’s, but I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern where I can’t organize tasks or manage my time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to complete tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I get distracted sometimes, sure, but I haven’t noticed it as a regular problem where I can’t finish tasks because of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so. The restlessness and fidgeting are what I notice most. Like when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot is bouncing under the desk, or I’m clicking a pen, or shifting around so much that I lose my place in what I’m reading. It’s not so much that I don’t understand what I’m reading—it’s more that I can’t keep still while I’m doing it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep finding myself getting up when I’m supposed to stay seated. Like during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even book club—I just can’t seem to sit there and listen the whole time. People are starting to notice, and it’s becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're seated during these events?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. If I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot is usually bouncing under the desk, or I’m clicking a pen without realizing it. I also shift around in my chair a lot, and sometimes I move so much I lose my place in what I’m reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do that a lot. If I overhear junior detectives talking through a lead, or even a neighbor chatting in the hallway, I’ll jump in with questions or advice before anyone asks me. Then afterward I realize I kind of barged into someone else’s conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself intruding on others physically, such as cutting in line or interrupting people's activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do intrude on people’s activities, mostly by jumping into conversations. Like if I hear junior detectives talking through a lead, or a neighbor talking in the hallway, I’ll cut in with questions or advice before they’ve asked me. Then afterward I realize I barged into something that wasn’t really my conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty waiting your turn in lines or queues?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Lines can annoy me, but I can’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern of having trouble waiting my turn in queues specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a major issue for me. I mean, some days are messy, like anyone’s, but I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern where I can’t organize tasks or manage my time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to complete tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I get distracted sometimes, sure, but I haven’t noticed it as a regular problem where I can’t finish tasks because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes when doing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I make mistakes sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed them being careless mistakes more than other people would.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble paying attention to details when working on a task?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, details can slip by me occasionally, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say it’s a frequent problem when I’m working on something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have trouble controlling your impulses in general, not just when it comes to interrupting conversations or intruding on others?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as having a general problem with impulses, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean. I can act quickly sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as something out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is more about not being able to sit still and keep quiet, or about having trouble paying attention to what's going on?\n\nPatient: It’s more the not being able to sit still and stay quiet, I think. What’s been bringing me in is that I keep getting up when I’m supposed to stay put—like during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even book club. People are starting to notice that I can’t just sit there and listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are the main issues you're dealing with, rather than problems with paying attention or controlling your impulses?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. The restlessness and fidgeting are what I notice most. Like when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot is bouncing under the desk, or I’m clicking a pen, or shifting around so much that I lose my place in what I’m reading. It’s not so much that I don’t understand what I’m reading—it’s more that I can’t keep still while I’m doing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s not so much that I don’t understand what I’m reading—it’s more that I can’t keep still while I’m doing it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often talk excessively, even when it's not your turn or when others are trying to speak?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / private_detective_or_investigator / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Talking\nmanifestation: \"I catch myself talking through every angle of a case or a client meeting until I've basically filled all the space, even when the other person is giving me clear signs they're done.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

Like when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot is bouncing under the desk, or I’m clicking a pen, or shifting around so much that I lose my place in what I’m reading. It’s not so much that I don’t understand what I’m reading—it’s more that I can’t keep still while I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, even when it's not your turn or when others are trying to speak?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. In client meetings or when I’m talking through a case, I’ll keep going through every angle out loud, and then I realize I’ve basically taken over the whole conversation. Sometimes the other person is already giving signs they’re done or wants to speak, and I’m still talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I can usually follow what’s going on, especially if it’s something important. I haven’t noticed attention being the main problem the way the sitting still and talking too much are. Sometimes I lose my place, but I think that’s more because I’m physically restless than because I can’t pay attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your excessive talking and interrupting others is more related to not being able to control what you're saying or because you're just really excited to share your thoughts?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I don’t stop myself in time. Like if I hear junior detectives talking through a lead, or even a neighbor talking out in the hallway, I’ll jump in with questions or advice before anyone asked me. In the moment it feels like I’m just trying to help or add something, but afterward I realize I barged into someone else’s conversation.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Lines can annoy me, but I can’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern of having trouble waiting my turn in queues specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a major issue for me. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep finding myself getting up when I’m supposed to stay seated. Like during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even book club—I just can’t seem to sit there and listen the whole time. People are starting to notice, and it’s becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're seated during these events?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. If I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot is usually bouncing under the desk, or I’m clicking a pen without realizing it. I also shift around in my chair a lot, and sometimes I move so much I lose my place in what I’m reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do that a lot. If I overhear junior detectives talking through a lead, or even a neighbor chatting in the hallway, I’ll jump in with questions or advice before anyone asks me. Then afterward I realize I kind of barged into someone else’s conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself intruding on others physically, such as cutting in line or interrupting people's activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do intrude on people’s activities, mostly by jumping into conversations. Like if I hear junior detectives talking through a lead, or a neighbor talking in the hallway, I’ll cut in with questions or advice before they’ve asked me. Then afterward I realize I barged into something that wasn’t really my conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty waiting your turn in lines or queues?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Lines can annoy me, but I can’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern of having trouble waiting my turn in queues specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a major issue for me. I mean, some days are messy, like anyone’s, but I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern where I can’t organize tasks or manage my time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to complete tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I get distracted sometimes, sure, but I haven’t noticed it as a regular problem where I can’t finish tasks because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes when doing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I make mistakes sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed them being careless mistakes more than other people would.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble paying attention to details when working on a task?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, details can slip by me occasionally, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say it’s a frequent problem when I’m working on something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have trouble controlling your impulses in general, not just when it comes to interrupting conversations or intruding on others?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as having a general problem with impulses, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean. I can act quickly sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as something out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is more about not being able to sit still and keep quiet, or about having trouble paying attention to what's going on?\n\nPatient: It’s more the not being able to sit still and stay quiet, I think. What’s been bringing me in is that I keep getting up when I’m supposed to stay put—like during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even book club. People are starting to notice that I can’t just sit there and listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are the main issues you're dealing with, rather than problems with paying attention or controlling your impulses?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. The restlessness and fidgeting are what I notice most. Like when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot is bouncing under the desk, or I’m clicking a pen, or shifting around so much that I lose my place in what I’m reading. It’s not so much that I don’t understand what I’m reading—it’s more that I can’t keep still while I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, even when it's not your turn or when others are trying to speak?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. In client meetings or when I’m talking through a case, I’ll keep going through every angle out loud, and then I realize I’ve basically taken over the whole conversation. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so. My grade-school report cards always had some version of “bright, but can’t stay in her seat” or “needs to wait her turn.” My mother still jokes that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So… yeah, it goes back pretty far.

If I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot is usually bouncing under the desk, or I’m clicking a pen without realizing it. I also shift around in my chair a lot, and sometimes I move so much I lose my place in what I’m reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do that a lot. If I overhear junior detectives talking through a lead, or even a neighbor chatting in the hallway, I’ll jump in with questions or advice before anyone asks me. Then afterward I realize I kind of barged into someone else’s conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself intruding on others physically, such as cutting in line or interrupting people's activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do intrude on people’s activities, mostly by jumping into conversations. Like if I hear junior detectives talking through a lead, or a neighbor talking in the hallway, I’ll cut in with questions or advice before they’ve asked me. Then afterward I realize I barged into something that wasn’t really my conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty waiting your turn in lines or queues?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Lines can annoy me, but I can’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern of having trouble waiting my turn in queues specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a major issue for me. I mean, some days are messy, like anyone’s, but I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern where I can’t organize tasks or manage my time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to complete tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I get distracted sometimes, sure, but I haven’t noticed it as a regular problem where I can’t finish tasks because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes when doing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I make mistakes sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed them being careless mistakes more than other people would.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble paying attention to details when working on a task?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, details can slip by me occasionally, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say it’s a frequent problem when I’m working on something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have trouble controlling your impulses in general, not just when it comes to interrupting conversations or intruding on others?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as having a general problem with impulses, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean. I can act quickly sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as something out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is more about not being able to sit still and keep quiet, or about having trouble paying attention to what's going on?\n\nPatient: It’s more the not being able to sit still and stay quiet, I think. What’s been bringing me in is that I keep getting up when I’m supposed to stay put—like during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even book club. People are starting to notice that I can’t just sit there and listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are the main issues you're dealing with, rather than problems with paying attention or controlling your impulses?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. The restlessness and fidgeting are what I notice most. Like when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot is bouncing under the desk, or I’m clicking a pen, or shifting around so much that I lose my place in what I’m reading. It’s not so much that I don’t understand what I’m reading—it’s more that I can’t keep still while I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, even when it's not your turn or when others are trying to speak?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. In client meetings or when I’m talking through a case, I’ll keep going through every angle out loud, and then I realize I’ve basically taken over the whole conversation. Sometimes the other person is already giving signs they’re done or wants to speak, and I’m still talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I can usually follow what’s going on, especially if it’s something important. I haven’t noticed attention being the main problem the way the sitting still and talking too much are. Sometimes I lose my place, but I think that’s more because I’m physically restless than because I can’t pay attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your excessive talking and interrupting others is more related to not being able to control what you're saying or because you're just really excited to share your thoughts?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I don’t stop myself in time. Like if I hear junior detectives talking through a lead, or even a neighbor talking out in the hallway, I’ll jump in with questions or advice before anyone asked me. In the moment it feels like I’m just trying to help or add something, but afterward I realize I barged into someone else’s conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main issue is more about acting on impulse without thinking or about having too much energy and activity?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the energy and activity, at least a lot of the time. If I don’t have field work that day, I still can’t really just be off duty. I’ll go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing case files to running errands, almost like I’m still chasing a lead. It’s hard for me to just sit and be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your problems with restlessness and fidgeting are worse when you're doing tasks that require a lot of mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely worse with computer work or anything like digging through records. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. It’s starting to cost me at work. I’ve missed details in investigations because I’m moving too fast or not sitting with things long enough, and some clients get irritated when I cut in or push the conversation along.

It’s also affected the team. A couple of junior detectives don’t seem as willing to debrief with me because they feel like I steamroll them. That’s been hard to hear.

Lines can annoy me, but I can’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern of having trouble waiting my turn in queues specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a major issue for me. I mean, some days are messy, like anyone’s, but I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern where I can’t organize tasks or manage my time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to complete tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I get distracted sometimes, sure, but I haven’t noticed it as a regular problem where I can’t finish tasks because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes when doing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I make mistakes sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed them being careless mistakes more than other people would.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble paying attention to details when working on a task?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, details can slip by me occasionally, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say it’s a frequent problem when I’m working on something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have trouble controlling your impulses in general, not just when it comes to interrupting conversations or intruding on others?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as having a general problem with impulses, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean. I can act quickly sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as something out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is more about not being able to sit still and keep quiet, or about having trouble paying attention to what's going on?\n\nPatient: It’s more the not being able to sit still and stay quiet, I think. What’s been bringing me in is that I keep getting up when I’m supposed to stay put—like during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even book club. People are starting to notice that I can’t just sit there and listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are the main issues you're dealing with, rather than problems with paying attention or controlling your impulses?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. The restlessness and fidgeting are what I notice most. Like when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot is bouncing under the desk, or I’m clicking a pen, or shifting around so much that I lose my place in what I’m reading. It’s not so much that I don’t understand what I’m reading—it’s more that I can’t keep still while I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, even when it's not your turn or when others are trying to speak?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. In client meetings or when I’m talking through a case, I’ll keep going through every angle out loud, and then I realize I’ve basically taken over the whole conversation. Sometimes the other person is already giving signs they’re done or wants to speak, and I’m still talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I can usually follow what’s going on, especially if it’s something important. I haven’t noticed attention being the main problem the way the sitting still and talking too much are. Sometimes I lose my place, but I think that’s more because I’m physically restless than because I can’t pay attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your excessive talking and interrupting others is more related to not being able to control what you're saying or because you're just really excited to share your thoughts?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I don’t stop myself in time. Like if I hear junior detectives talking through a lead, or even a neighbor talking out in the hallway, I’ll jump in with questions or advice before anyone asked me. In the moment it feels like I’m just trying to help or add something, but afterward I realize I barged into someone else’s conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main issue is more about acting on impulse without thinking or about having too much energy and activity?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the energy and activity, at least a lot of the time. If I don’t have field work that day, I still can’t really just be off duty. I’ll go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing case files to running errands, almost like I’m still chasing a lead. It’s hard for me to just sit and be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your problems with restlessness and fidgeting are worse when you're doing tasks that require a lot of mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely worse with computer work or anything like digging through records. If I’m stuck at the desk reading for a while, my foot is bouncing, I’m clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes I move so much I lose my place in what I’m reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are more noticeable when you are in quiet or low-stimulation environments?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. When it’s quiet, it almost stands out more. Like yoga used to help me settle down, or I’d go to book club and read along, but now I’ll catch myself whispering little comments, rustling papers, or looking for a reason to get up and move around. It’s hard to just be still in those settings.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this restless and fidgety, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. My grade-school report cards always had some version of “bright, but can’t stay in her seat” or “needs to wait her turn.” My mother still jokes that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. That’s been hard to hear.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
